Sighted users learn the affordance after one tap. **Why honor Spencer's direction:** the request is explicit, the trade-off is small (one tap to learn), and the convention is at least defensible as "the icon names the current state" rather than "the icon names the destination." Plenty of toggle affordances elsewhere on iOS use the current-state convention (e.g. the Now Playing bar's play/pause button shows the current play state — tapping pause when playing, play when paused — which IS the destination convention; but the Photos app's grid/aspect-fit toggle shows the current grid type). The two conventions coexist in the iOS ecosystem; this app picks the current-state one with Spencer's explicit blessing.

  **(2) Considered alternatives.** **(a) Destination-icon convention (typical iOS).** Show `list.bullet` while in gallery (telegraphs "switch to list"), `square.grid.2x2` while in list. Rejected per Spencer's explicit direction — the user asked for the current-state convention by name. If a future user-feedback signal shows the current-state convention confuses users, the icon mapping is a one-line swap inside `RecipeListLayout`'s `toggleIconName` computed property; no AC change, no schema change, no analytics impact. **(b) Segmented control (`Picker` with `.segmented` style) above the list instead of a nav-bar button.** Rejected — segmented controls take screen real estate from the existing `FilterChipRow` (Search) and offline banner (Feed); a nav-bar button is the lower-visual-weight affordance for a two-state toggle that users won't flip often. The segmented control also doesn't compose cleanly with the gear icon AC-32.1 places on the Recipes-tab trailing edge — the gear lives in the nav bar so the toggle should too. **(c) Settings-page row instead of an in-tab control.** Rejected — the layout choice is a "current browsing intent" preference (am I scanning many or savoring few?) rather than a "permanent preference" the user sets once. In-tab placement matches the user's mental model (I'm here, I want this layout NOW). Settings-page rows are the right surface for stable preferences (metric units, appearance, notifications); the layout toggle is closer to a list filter than a global preference. **(d) Per-tab persistence (separate `@AppStorage` keys for FeedView's and SearchView's layout).** Rejected — Spencer's round-7 wording ("Apply the same convention to BOTH the Recipes & Articles tab AND the Search tab") explicitly framed it as one shared preference. The user-experience argument: if I prefer list view, I want it everywhere I'm scanning recipes (Feed + Search are both "list of recipe rows" surfaces); fragmenting the preference would create a "why is it list here but gallery there?" surprise. **(e) Three-mode toggle (gallery / list / compact).** Rejected — Spencer asked for two modes; adding a third would require a new visual variant of `RecipeCard` and a new AC for what the third mode looks like. The two-mode framing is bounded and shippable; a third mode is a future backlog item if user-feedback calls for it. **(f) Apply the same toggle to the Categories tab + Saved tab.** Rejected for v1 of T-650 — Spencer's framing named the Recipes & Articles tab + Search tab specifically; Categories has a different list affordance (it's a list-of-categories, not a list-of-recipes), and Saved would inherit the toggle naturally as a third consumer but the round-7 ask was Feed + Search. If T-650's pattern lands well, extending to Saved is a one-PR follow-up.

  **(3) Persistence + shared state shape.** `@AppStorage("dod.recipeListLayout")` storing the raw value of a `RecipeListLayout` enum (`"gallery"` / `"list"`). Default is `.gallery` — both for new installs (the key is absent) and for users migrating from a pre-T-650 install (the same absent-key fallback). The default preserves CC-9's 2-column grid behavior byte-for-byte; users who never tap the toggle see no change. The flag round-trips through `UserDefaults.standard` so the L1 unit tests can assert the round-trip with an isolated `UserDefaults(suiteName:)` — the same pattern `RecentSearches` and `SettingsViewModel` already use. **Why not `dod.settings.recipeListLayout` under the Settings prefix?** The toggle is an in-tab control, not a Settings-page row; consistent with the AC-38.2 framing, it lives in the `dod.*` namespace but outside the `dod.settings.*` sub-namespace. **Why expose the enum in `DODDesignSystem` and not in `DODFeatureFeed`?** `SearchView` lives in `DODFeatureSearch`; if the enum were in `DODFeatureFeed`, `DODFeatureSearch` would need a feature-package dependency on `DODFeatureFeed` (which would be a layering violation — feature packages don't depend on each other). `DODDesignSystem` is the shared dependency both feature packages already pull in, so hosting the enum + the `@AppStorage` key + the toggle icon mapping there gives both views the same building blocks without cross-feature coupling.

  **(4) List-row visual specification.** `RecipeCard.ListRow` is a new `View` in `Packages/DODDesignSystem/Sources/DODDesignSystem/Components/RecipeCard.swift` (sibling to the existing `RecipeCard` struct, not nested — Swift's `struct` extension surface keeps the file organized without forcing a sub-namespace). Layout: `HStack(alignment: .center, spacing: DODSpacing.sm)` with three children — a 60×60pt `AsyncImage` thumbnail clipped to `RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: DODSpacing.xs)`, a `VStack(alignment: .leading, spacing: DODSpacing.xxs)` carrying the title (`DODType.heading`, `.lineLimit(1)`) and excerpt (`DODType.caption`, `.lineLimit(1)`), and an optional trailing time chip (only when `totalTimeDisplay` is non-nil — same compact pill the gallery card uses, reused via a shared `timeChip(_:)` helper). The row's vertical padding is `DODSpacing.sm` top/bottom. The background is `DODColor.surfaceElevated` (same token the gallery card uses) inside a `RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: DODSpacing.sm)`. The `recipeCardTap` + `recipeCardContextMenu` modifiers compose on the row the same way they do on the gallery card — no AC-34.1 regression. A `Divider` between adjacent rows would clash with the per-row rounded background; instead the rows are visually separated by `DODSpacing.xs` vertical spacing inside the enclosing `LazyVStack`. **Why a fresh `LazyVStack` and not a `List`?** Spencer's framing mentioned `List`, but using `List` inside the existing screen layout would force the scroll geometry to change (List embeds its own scroll view + provides its own row insets + has different swipe-action semantics + interacts oddly with the `OfflineBanner` overlay AC-2/CC-2 mandates). `LazyVStack` inside the existing `ScrollView` is the lower-churn substitution — same scroll geometry as the gallery, same `OfflineBanner` z-ordering, same pull-to-refresh semantics. The behavioral spec ("denser rows for quick scanning") is satisfied by the row layout itself; the container choice is implementation detail.

  **(5) Toolbar placement on FeedView.** AC-32.1 already places the gear icon on the trailing edge of FeedView's nav bar via `.topBarTrailing`. T-650 adds a second toolbar item on the same trailing edge — the layout toggle button — and orders them so the toggle sits to the **leading side of the gear**, keeping the gear at the absolute trailing edge. SwiftUI's `ToolbarItem` ordering inside a single `ToolbarItemGroup(placement: .topBarTrailing)` follows declaration order from leading to trailing, so the toggle is declared first and the gear second. **Considered:** placing the toggle on the **leading** edge of the nav bar (where `Image(systemName: "magnifyingglass")` would sit in apps with a search-button leading affordance). Rejected — the leading edge is unused on FeedView today (no back button at the root of the tab's NavigationStack), and putting one isolated control on the leading edge while the gear sits trailing would create asymmetric visual weight. Keeping both trailing-edge controls grouped is the lower-churn placement. **Why not put the toggle in the nav-bar title area (replacing the navigation title text with a segmented control)?** Rejected — would conflict with US-37 / AC-37.1 ("Recipes & Articles" title text) and would not generalize to Search (whose title is "Search"). The trailing-edge button placement works identically on both tabs.

  **(6) Toolbar placement on SearchView.** SearchView pre-T-650 has no toolbar items — the search field, filter chips, and content live in the body. T-650 adds a `ToolbarItem(placement: .topBarTrailing)` carrying the layout toggle button. The nav title remains "Search" per existing behavior. The same `topBarTrailing` placement is used so the toggle sits in the same visual location on both tabs (a user who learns the affordance on Feed finds it in the same spot on Search). On macOS (which the iOS-feature packages also build against for test slices), `.topBarTrailing` doesn't exist; the fallback uses `.automatic` (the same `#if os(iOS)` pattern FeedView already uses for the gear icon per AC-32.1's existing implementation in `FeedView.swift:60`).

  **(7) Accessibility.** Per AC-38.1, the accessibility label reads "Layout, gallery" / "Layout, list" (current state) with the action hint "switch to list" / "switch to gallery" (destination) — VoiceOver users hear both, so the icon-convention deviation doesn't hide the affordance's destination. Both the gallery and list layouts pass the existing CC-1 accessibility cross-cutting AC: every interactive element has a VoiceOver label, Dynamic Type up to AX5 is respected (the list-row layout's `DODType.heading` + `DODType.caption` are already Dynamic-Type-aware), contrast meets WCAG AA in both light + dark per the existing `DODColor.label` / `DODColor.labelSecondary` token contracts. The `RecipeCard.ListRow` thumbnail's 60pt fixed size is intentionally NOT Dynamic-Type-scaled — the row's text content grows with the user's preferred content size category and the image stays a fixed visual anchor (matches iOS Mail's row layout, which keeps avatars fixed-size while subject text grows).

  **(8) Test pyramid impact.** **L1:** `RecipeListLayoutTests` in `DODDesignSystemTests` (new — round-trip the enum's raw value through an isolated `UserDefaults`, assert default `.gallery` on absent key, assert defensive fallback to `.gallery` on a malformed string value). **L4:** `FeedViewSnapshotTests` and `SearchViewSnapshotTests` each gain a `_listLayout_light` and `_listLayout_dark` baseline (the existing `_loadedFeed_*` / `_searchResults_*` baselines continue to lock the `.gallery` default — they're unchanged because the default is unchanged). `DesignSystemSnapshotTests` gains a `recipeCard_listRow_full` + `recipeCard_listRow_noTimeChip` baseline pair (mirror of the existing `recipeCard_full` / `recipeCard_noTimeChip` baselines, but for the new row variant). No re-recording of existing baselines is needed because the default layout is `.gallery` and existing snapshot fixtures don't set `@AppStorage("dod.recipeListLayout")` — `record: .missing` lays the new baselines down on the first iOS-sim test run.

  **(9) What CL-64 explicitly does NOT touch:** the `RecipeRoute.recipe(item:)` grammar (the row tap routes through the same `onSelect` callback the gallery card uses), the deep-link grammar, the widget surfaces (`WidgetCard` and its siblings are unchanged — widget layouts are fixed by the system widget family, not by this in-app toggle), the analytics events (no `layoutChanged` event), the persistence schema (`@AppStorage` is `UserDefaults`-backed, not SwiftData), the `RecipeStore` API, the search index, the Saved tab's layout (this CL is bounded to Feed + Search per Spencer's framing), the Categories tab's `.insetGrouped` list (different surface, different affordance — the toggle's two layouts are designed for the recipe-row content, not the category-row content), the gear-icon position on FeedView (AC-32.1's `.topBarTrailing` placement preserved — the toggle sits to the leading side of the gear in the same group), the existing AC-1.3 list row format for the `.gallery` case (byte-identical to today). The `RecipeCard` struct itself is unchanged — `RecipeCard.ListRow` is a sibling, not a replacement.

  **(10) Coordination.** T-650 lands after T-620, T-630, and T-640 merge (per the round-7 sequencing — T-650 is queued because it touches `FeedView` heavily). T-620 owns `Packages/DODFeatureRecipeDetail/**`; T-630 owns `SettingsView` + `SettingsViewModel` + `RecipeStore+ImageCache` + `TelemetryDeckTransport` + `RootView`; T-640 owns `AppTab` + `FeedView` nav title + `ArticleDetailView` + `PostKind`. T-650 touches `FeedView` (adding a toolbar item alongside the gear icon T-630 + T-640 preserved), `SearchView` (adding a toolbar item from scratch), `RecipeCard.swift` (adding the `ListRow` variant alongside the existing card), and the spec/clarifications/tasks/backlog files. Source-side collisions zero with T-620 / T-630; T-640's `FeedView.navigationTitle("Recipes & Articles")` rename is preserved; only the toolbar block grows by one ToolbarItem. T-640's coordination note ("T-650 is queued for after T-640 merges because T-650 also touches `FeedView` heavily") is honored.

  **Implementing PR:** T-650 (`feat/T-650-gallery-list-toggle`). **Coordination note:** No parallel agents running per the round-7 task framing. The three prior round-7 PRs (T-620 / T-630 / T-640) have all merged before T-650 starts.

- **CL-65 (US-37 / T-660, 2026-05-27)** — **Tab-bar label "Recipes & Articles" truncates on iPhone widths; split tab label from nav title so the bottom bar reads short "Recipes" and the screen header keeps the full "Recipes & Articles".** T-640 (US-37 / CL-63) renamed the Recipes tab to "Recipes & Articles" in both `AppTab.title` (which drove the bottom-tab `Label(...)` AND `FeedView.navigationTitle` from a single string) and `FeedView.navigationTitle` (same string, same value — `AppTab.feed.title`). The rename worked correctly for the screen header but introduced a user-visible regression at the bottom of the screen: the system tab bar item has a fixed-width slot (~80pt on standard iPhone widths in portrait at default Dynamic Type), and "Recipes & Articles" renders with an ellipsis as "Recipes & Arti..." Anyone using the app sees the truncated string every time they look at the bottom of the screen, on every screen, in every tab — it's a high-impression-count regression even if functionally benign. The fix is to decouple the two strings: introduce a new `AppTab.tabLabel: String` computed property returning a tab-bar-appropriate short string per case (matches `title` for every case except `.feed`, which returns "Recipes"); update `RootView.phoneTabs`'s `TabView` block to read `tabLabel` for the `Label("...", systemImage: ...)` declaration; leave `title` and `FeedView.navigationTitle("Recipes & Articles")` unchanged so the screen header keeps the full content semantics. **This CL amends AC-37.1.** The original AC-37.1 wording (which described `AppTab.title` driving both the tab label AND the nav title) is preserved with strike-through-style amendment language pointing back to this CL.

  **(1) Why "Recipes" (the pre-T-640 wording) and not a different short label.** The two real candidates are (a) "Recipes" (the pre-T-640 label — short, unambiguously paired with the `house` glyph, zero net change vs. shipping pre-T-640) and (b) "Articles" (the new wording — but the icon is `house` and the bottom-bar pair "house + Articles" reads as "home + articles" which is a worse mental model than "home + Recipes"). "Recipes" wins on both fit (7 chars vs 8) and icon-pairing semantics. The full content semantics — that this tab surfaces BOTH recipes AND articles — are conveyed by the screen header (`FeedView.navigationTitle` shows "Recipes & Articles" the moment the user taps the tab) and by every list cell that mixes recipe + article rows. The tab bar label's job is to point the user at the right tab; the content-typing nuance lives on the screen, not on the 11pt bottom-bar pill.

  **(2) Considered alternatives.** **(a) Abbreviate the full string itself.** "Recipes & Art." or "Recipes/Art." or "Recipes+Art." Rejected — every abbreviation reads worse than the standalone "Recipes" word and introduces a punctuation choice (`/`, `+`, `.`, `&`) that doesn't carry meaning. The pre-T-640 label is a known-good string with zero risk. **(b) Use SwiftUI's `.minimumScaleFactor(...)` on the tab item to let the system shrink the label.** Rejected — `Label` inside `.tabItem { }` doesn't honor view modifiers the way a regular `Text` does; the system tab bar renders the label string at the system-standard tab-bar font size (~11pt) regardless of view-tree modifiers, and even if `minimumScaleFactor` did fire the scaled-down text would be illegibly small at AX5 Dynamic Type. **(c) Drop the tab label entirely — show only the icon.** Rejected — every other tab has a label, and dropping just one would create asymmetric visual weight. Also tab-bar labels are an accessibility affordance (VoiceOver reads the label as the tab's name); dropping it would hurt screen-reader users. **(d) Choose a different short word — "Home", "Feed", "Browse".** Rejected — "Home" conflicts with the `house` glyph's literal meaning but isn't the product's name for the surface; "Feed" is a generic term that doesn't telegraph "recipes" as concretely as "Recipes" does; "Browse" is too verb-y for a noun-noun-noun-noun tab grouping (Recipes / Categories / Saved / Search are all nouns). **(e) Use two-line labels (some iOS tab bars wrap to a second line).** Rejected — SwiftUI's `Label` in a `.tabItem` doesn't natively wrap to two lines on iPhone (it wraps on iPad regular size class but not in compact); attempting to force a `\n` into the label string just renders as a literal `\n`. **(f) Change the iPad sidebar `Label(tab.title, systemImage: tab.systemImage)` to also read `tabLabel`.** Rejected — the iPad sidebar uses a `List` row, not a fixed-width tab item; the sidebar has plenty of horizontal space to render "Recipes & Articles" without truncation, and showing "Recipes" there would be a regression vs. T-640's full content semantics. The fix is bounded to `RootView.phoneTabs`'s `TabView` declaration — `iPadSplit`'s sidebar list keeps reading `tab.title`. **(g) Apply the split convention proactively to every tab (have `tabLabel` differ from `title` for every case).** Rejected — only `.feed` has a long enough `title` to actually truncate. The fallthrough `tabLabel == title` for every other case keeps the property minimal — anyone reading `AppTab.swift` sees that only one tab has divergent strings, with a comment explaining why.

  **(3) Why a new property and not just shorter `title`.** Three reasons. **First**, `FeedView.navigationTitle("Recipes & Articles")` is consumed at the screen-header level where there's plenty of horizontal space, and the full content-semantics framing ("the tab surfaces BOTH recipes AND articles") is exactly what the user should read on a 36pt large-title nav bar — shortening `title` would lose that user-facing context everywhere it matters. **Second**, AC-37.1's existing wording explicitly named two surfaces ("`AppTab.title` (drives the bottom-tab label per SwiftUI's `Label(systemImage:)` convention) and `FeedView.navigationTitle` (drives the screen's nav-bar title)") — the spec described two consumers of one string; T-660 splits that into two strings for two consumers without changing either consumer's surface. **Third**, downstream consumers that may reference `title` for screen-header purposes (e.g. iPad sidebar, future hypothetical screen-level adornment) should not be forced to switch to `tabLabel` — the property semantics are clearly differentiated: `title` is the full content-typed name of the tab, `tabLabel` is the bottom-bar-fitted short version. Most tabs have them equal; the `.feed` case is the only one that needs the split today.

  **(4) Telemetry impact.** None. AC-16.4's `telemetryName` (the stable funnel-comparison identifier) is `.feed → "feed"` and doesn't read `title` or `tabLabel`. Telemetry events fire on tab selection (`screenView(name: tab.telemetryName)`) and the funnel data continues to flow byte-identical across the T-640 → T-660 transition.

  **(5) Test impact.** Re-record any L4 snapshot baselines that capture the bottom tab bar at iPhone widths — most notably `Packages/DODDesignSystem/Tests/DODDesignSystemTests/__Snapshots__/TabBarSnapshotTests/*.png` (US-16 / AC-16.5's eight iPhone 13 + iPad 12.9" baselines). The iPad baselines render the same fixture but on a 1024pt-wide canvas where the tab bar has more horizontal slack; if the iPad rendering is byte-identical to today (which it should be — iPad split-view replaces the tab bar with a sidebar, but the snapshot fixture renders the `TabView` directly to validate the visual identity), those baselines stay byte-identical. The iPhone baselines re-record (the tab label string for `.feed` flips from a placeholder "Recipes" — the snapshot fixture's local `Tab.feed.title` was still "Recipes" pre-T-660 because T-640 didn't update the snapshot fixture to mirror the new wording; T-660 keeps the fixture string at "Recipes" too, which now matches the new `AppTab.feed.tabLabel`). The fixture-to-source-of-truth coupling pinned by `DODAppUnitTests/AppTabTests` and `SmokeTests.test_tabBarOrderMatchesSpec` is realigned in T-660 by the fact that both tests look for the bottom-tab label "Recipes" (which is now sourced from `AppTab.feed.tabLabel`) rather than "Recipes & Articles" (which would have been the post-T-640 expectation if `AppTab.title` drove the tab item — but the tests were never updated to that, so they were already aligned with the "Recipes" wording, just for the wrong reason; T-660 makes the alignment correct-for-the-right-reason). No new L1 unit test is strictly required — the new `tabLabel` property's per-case mapping is trivial enough that the existing `AppTabTests.test_titles_matchSpec` style of assertion suffices as a regression guard; adding `test_tabLabels_matchSpec` is bounded and worth doing for the same casual-rename protection rationale `test_titles_matchSpec` already documents.

  **(6) What CL-65 explicitly does NOT touch:** AC-37.2..AC-37.8 (PostKind classification, ArticleDetailView rendering, article save semantics, regression pins — all preserved as-is because none reference the tab-bar label string), the article-rendering path (`RecipeDetailViewModel.fetchAndParse()`, `ArticleBodyExtractor`, the `articleBodyHTML` SwiftData column, the `markJSONLDFailed` kind discriminator — all preserved), AC-16.1's tab order (Recipes & Articles → Categories → Saved → Search — `AppTab.allCases` ordering unchanged), AC-16.4 (telemetry names stable), AC-16.5's iPad sidebar (the `Label(tab.title, systemImage: tab.systemImage)` in `iPadSplit`'s sidebar `List` keeps reading the full `title` — sidebar rows have plenty of horizontal space and the content-semantics framing is appropriate there), CL-63's `PostKind` mapping (`Recipe.kind: PostKind`, `.recipe` / `.article` cases), the `house` glyph (`AppTab.feed.systemImage`), the `RecipeRoute.recipe(item:)` grammar, the deep-link grammar (`dod://recipe/<id>`), the widget surfaces (widget faces have their own labels, not driven by `AppTab.title`/`tabLabel`), the analytics events.

  **Spec/trace:** US-37 / AC-37.1 (amended in this CL); CL-63 (preserved — the broader article-rendering decision is unaffected); AC-16.1, AC-16.4, AC-16.5 (preserved — tab ordering, telemetry stability, snapshot baseline coverage). Implementing PR: T-660 (`fix/T-660-tab-label-truncation`). **Coordination note:** T-670 (phantom searches), T-680 (Shopping List), T-690 (Voice Mode) running in parallel on different files. T-660 touches `App/AppTab.swift` (new `tabLabel` property) and `App/RootView.swift` (one-line `Label(tab.tabLabel, ...)` swap inside `phoneTabs`'s `TabView`). Source-side collisions: zero with T-670 / T-680 / T-690 (T-660 owns `App/AppTab.swift` + `App/RootView.swift`'s `phoneTabs` block exclusively; the three parallels touch different files in different packages). Spec-file collisions on `clarifications.md` / `spec.md` / `tasks.md` / `backlog.md` are expected at merge time and the rebaser picks the next free CL/US/T slot (T-660 reserves CL-65, T-660 — no new US is needed because the AC amendment lives under the existing US-37 banner).

- **CL-66 (US-29 / T-670, 2026-05-27)** — **"Clear All" left three curated terms ("Bourbon", "Sweet Potato", "Brisket") in the Recent section. Root cause: tapping a "Try" suggestion pill routed through the same `recents.record(...)` path as user-typed queries, AND `clearRecentSearches()` did not cancel in-flight debounced searches.** Round-7's `SearchView.onCategoryTap` set `viewModel.query = category.name` directly, which fired `query.didSet → scheduleSearch() → performSearch()`, and `performSearch()` called `recents.record(trimmed)` on completion regardless of how the query was originated. So a user tapping the "Bourbon" / "Sweet Potato" / "Brisket" curated pills in the idle empty state silently persisted those terms into the recent-searches store — terms the user never typed. Compounding the leak: tapping "Clear All" called `RecentSearches.clear()` + reset `recentSearches = []`, but did NOT cancel any in-flight `debounceTask`; a debounced `performSearch` that started just before the tap (typical with a 300ms debounce + a tap that lands fast after a pill tap) completed *after* the wipe and re-recorded the same term, making Clear All look broken. Fix: (a) add `SearchViewModel.selectCuratedSuggestion(_:)` which sets `query = name` and flips a new `queryFromCuratedTap` flag; `performSearch()` skips `recents.record(...)` when the flag is set; (b) `clearRecentSearches()` calls `debounceTask?.cancel()` as a defensive belt against the in-flight race; (c) `SearchView.onCategoryTap` routes through `selectCuratedSuggestion(_:)` instead of raw `query = category.name`. The fix is bounded to `Packages/DODFeatureSearch/Sources/DODFeatureSearch/{SearchViewModel.swift,SearchView.swift}` + one L1 test. `IdleSuggestionsView` already conditions the Recent section on `if !recents.isEmpty` (no Try-pill fallback inside the Recent section); the bug never was a view-side leak, it was a recording-path leak. Locked by `DODFeatureSearchTests.SearchViewModelTests.curatedTapDoesNotRecordRecentAndClearAllLeavesRecentEmpty` (REG-19) — exercises three curated taps, asserts empty recents; types a real query, asserts that one recent persists; Clear All, asserts empty; one more curated tap, asserts still empty. **Spec/trace:** US-29 / AC-29.1 (curated "Try" tap routes through the query field — preserved), AC-29.2 (Clear All affordance — preserved + hardened against the in-flight race), CL-49 (single-source-of-truth routing through the view-model — preserved), REG-19 (the new test pin). Implementing PR: T-670 (`fix/T-670-phantom-recents-v2`). **Coordination note:** T-660 (tab-bar truncation), T-680 (Shopping List), T-690 (Voice Mode) running in parallel on different files; T-670 owns `Packages/DODFeatureSearch/**` exclusively. Spec-file collisions on `clarifications.md` / `spec.md` / `tasks.md` / `backlog.md` are expected at merge time; T-670 reserves CL-66, REG-19, T-670 — no new US (the behavior change lives under the existing US-29 banner).
---

## Phase 12 amendments (Shopping List, 2026-05-27)

US-39 / T-680 / T-681..T-689. Twelve design questions resolved as twelve clarifications. The Shopping List feature is a local-only addition to the Saved tab: ingredients from saved recipes get rolled up into an aisle-grouped, checkable, shareable list with no blog backend, no iCloud sync, no account. Every CL below documents one design decision plus the considered-and-rejected alternatives — written with the future-self lens that lets a contributor six months out understand *why* v1 chose this shape over the four-or-five plausible alternatives. The three explicit deferrals (named lists, pantry inventory, geofenced reminders) are documented in CL-72, CL-71, and CL-68 respectively so a future Tier-2 graduation has a starting point. Implementing cluster: T-680 (this spec PR) + T-681..T-689 (implementation PRs).

- **CL-67 (US-39 / T-681, 2026-05-27)** — **Aisle classifier strategy: ship a static keyword map for v1 (~80 entries hand-curated from a US grocery-store walk), with a documented escalation path to (b) ingredient-tagged dictionary if the `Aisle.other` share of `shoppingListItemAdded` events crosses a documented threshold.** The Shopping List feature classifies each ingredient line (e.g. `"2 cups diced yellow onion"`, `"1 tsp smoked paprika"`, `"1 lb ground beef, 80/20"`) into a coarse-grained `Aisle` enum (~9 cases: produce, dairy, meatSeafood, bakery, frozen, pantry, spices, beverages, other). The classifier is the only piece of the Shopping List that has a real algorithmic decision under it — every other surface is plumbing. **Four candidate strategies for v1:** **(a) Static keyword map** — a ~80-entry `[String: Aisle]` table mapping known ingredient stems ("onion" → produce, "milk" → dairy, "paprika" → spices, "ground beef" → meatSeafood, "vanilla extract" → pantry) plus the fallback rule `default → .other`. The classifier tokenizes the ingredient line (lowercase, drop quantity + unit prefix via a small regex `^\s*[\d¼½¾⅓⅔⅛⅜⅝⅞/.\s-]*(cups?|tsps?|tbsps?|oz|lbs?|grams?|g|ml|l|cloves?|sprigs?|cans?|packages?|pkgs?)?\s*`, drop trailing comma-separated qualifiers via `,.*$` split), then looks up each remaining whitespace-separated token against the keyword map and returns the first match (or `.other` if no match). **(b) Ingredient-tagged dictionary** — every ingredient line gets pre-tagged at the JSON-LD parse step (extend `RecipeIngredient` with an optional `aisle: Aisle?` field populated by a richer parser that handles compound terms like "all-purpose flour", "extra virgin olive oil", "boneless skinless chicken thighs" via a multi-token longest-prefix match against a ~200-entry dictionary). The classifier becomes a no-op pass-through when the field is populated. **(c) Hybrid** — try (a) first; on `.other`, fall through to a longer-form fuzzy matcher (Levenshtein distance ≤ 2 against the canonical-name table for known typos: "yelow onion" → "yellow onion" → produce). **(d) WP-side classification** — extend the WordPress backend so every published recipe carries an `aisle` post-meta per ingredient via a WPRM plugin or a custom REST namespace; the app reads the pre-classified value, the classifier is empty on-device. **Decision: ship (a) — the static keyword map — for v1.** **Why (a) and not (b):** (b) requires editing the JSON-LD parse path (every ingredient line gets a richer parser), changes the on-disk Codable shape of `RecipeIngredient` (a new optional `aisle` field) which forces a back-compat decode branch for older cached recipes, adds a new domain-level decision (where does the longest-prefix-match dictionary live? is it bundled with the app or fetched at first launch? what's the update path?), and is fundamentally a v1.x scope: it's the right answer once we have telemetry showing the static map's miss rate is too high. Until that signal exists, (b) is YAGNI. **Why (a) and not (c):** the fuzzy matcher in (c) is a flashing-warning-light pattern — fuzzy matching with Levenshtein on cooking ingredients introduces false positives ("rye flour" vs "rice flour" are one edit apart but route to different aisles in a real grocery store: rye to bakery, rice to pantry; the v1 static map handles both correctly as exact matches but the fuzzy path could flip them). Building a fuzzy matcher costs the same as building (b)'s richer dictionary but produces a worse result. Strike (c). **Why (a) and not (d):** (d) requires a WordPress-side change Spencer + dad haven't authorized, requires a constitution amendment §4 (the v1 app is "mostly read-only" and an aisle-classified ingredient would be a new write surface on the blog side even if not the app side), and bottlenecks aisle accuracy on Ned's content-author workflow rather than on the v1 app's release. Strike (d). **Escalation trigger for (a) → (b):** the `shoppingListItemAdded(recipeID:aisle:)` event per AC-39.10 records the classifier's output for every row inserted. If the share of `.other` rows exceeds **15%** of all inserted rows over a rolling 30-day window — the documented threshold below which the v1 classifier is "good enough" per the keyword-map design intent — that's the signal to plan (b). A separate task captures the dictionary build, the JSON-LD parse extension, the back-compat decode, and the deprecation of the on-device keyword map. The 15% threshold is a starting calibration; dad's actual cooking is the ground truth and we'll adjust on the first 30-day report. **Why ~80 entries and not 50 or 200:** 50 is too thin (drops common items like "yogurt", "ground turkey", "tomato paste"); 200 is the (b) dictionary size and the build cost crosses the YAGNI threshold for v1; ~80 covers every entry on the dad-tested DOD recipe corpus (sampled by walking the public dutchovendaddy.com recipe list and tokenizing the JSON-LD `recipeIngredient` arrays of the ~50 highest-traffic posts). **What the keyword map looks like:** the keyword map ships as a `String -> Aisle` static dictionary literal in `Packages/DODSupport/Sources/DODSupport/IngredientAisleClassifier.swift` (NOT as a JSON resource — the cost of a resource load + parse + cache vs. a static dictionary literal that the Swift compiler turns into a constant is not worth the extra moving part; the dictionary is small and human-readable in source). The table is grouped by aisle (alphabetical inside each aisle block) with one comment block per aisle explaining the curation rationale ("Produce includes onion, garlic, every leafy green, every common fruit, herbs as the *fresh* form — not dried; dried herbs go under Spices") so a future curator can extend the table without re-deriving the design intent. **What the classifier API looks like:** `public enum IngredientAisleClassifier { public static func classify(_ ingredientText: String) -> Aisle }` — pure, value-typed, deterministic, no dependencies on Foundation beyond `String.lowercased(with: .current)` (NOT `lowercased()` without a locale because Turkish dotted-i would break some non-ASCII edge cases — though the keyword map is ASCII-only, the input is user-facing recipe text and locale-aware lowercasing is the constitution §10 hygiene). **Considered:** ship without an aisle classifier (render the list as one flat alphabetical column, no grouping). Rejected — the round-3 backlog entry called out "grouped by aisle (produce / pantry / dairy / meat / spices / other)" as a load-bearing UX detail; a flat list is a much weaker affordance because the user can't quickly say "I'm in the produce section, what do I still need?" without scanning the entire list. The aisle classifier is the differentiator between "shopping list" and "ingredient memo." **Considered:** classify at insertion time vs. at render time. **Decision: at insertion time** — the `Aisle` value is stored on the `ShoppingListItem` row so the render path is a pure read-and-group; this also means the classifier output is stable across app launches even if the keyword map changes between releases (a row added today with the v1 keyword map stays at its v1 aisle until the user clears the list, which avoids confusing "my onion moved from Produce to Other after I updated the app" UX). **Considered:** make the classifier injectable (a protocol with a `LiveIngredientAisleClassifier` and `FakeIngredientAisleClassifier` per the DI pattern the rest of the app uses). **Decision: enum + static func, not a protocol** — the classifier has zero dependencies, zero side effects, and zero state; a protocol surface is over-engineering for a pure value-in-value-out function. Tests can call `IngredientAisleClassifier.classify(...)` directly. **Considered:** route the table through `Bundle.main.url(forResource: "ingredient-aisle-map", withExtension: "json")` and parse at first launch. Rejected — needless I/O, needless launch-time cost, needless decode error path. The table is small and ships in source. **Considered:** support per-user keyword-map customization ("add 'arepa flour' → bakery to my personal map"). Rejected — out of v1 scope; would require Settings UI, persistence, and a merge strategy with the canonical map. Future Tier-2 task if user signal emerges. **Spec/trace:** US-39 / AC-39.4 (aisle grouping render); AC-39.10 (telemetry surface that drives the (a) → (b) escalation trigger); AC-39.12 (no network egress — the classifier is pure on-device). Implementing PR: T-681 (`feat/T-681-shopping-list-domain-classifier`).

- **CL-68 (US-39 / T-680, 2026-05-27) — DEFERRED ITEM** — **Location-based notifications for grocery-store reminders are EXPLICITLY DEFERRED out of v1 of the Shopping List feature.** The round-3 capture mentioned this in passing as a "could be cool" — geofence the user's regular grocery store, push a "you've got 12 items on your shopping list" reminder when iOS detects they've entered the geofence radius. **Decision: do NOT graduate this into v1.** **Why deferred:** geofenced reminders require (a) `CLLocationManager` with `requestWhenInUseAuthorization()` plus an `NSLocationWhenInUseUsageDescription` Info.plist entry that meets App Store review's "clear and specific" copy rule (Apple has rejected vague location strings; the copy must be ingredient-list-specific: "Dutch Oven Daddy uses your location to remind you about your shopping list when you arrive at a grocery store you've added"), (b) a user flow to add and label a grocery store as a geofence center point (a map picker, a Place name field, a radius slider — at minimum), (c) `UNUserNotificationCenter` integration with location-trigger notifications (which is a different code path from time-trigger and from push), (d) an App Privacy questionnaire amendment to add "Coarse Location — App Functionality" under Apple's Location category (the constitution §9 mapping table doesn't currently include this — adding it requires re-running the App Store privacy questionnaire and is a release-prep step), (e) an L3 smoke test that the location-permission flow doesn't crash when the user denies (a real failure mode for any new permission), and (f) a meaningful background story for "what if the user adds five geofences? what if iOS suspends the app and the geofence event arrives while the app is terminated? what's the wake-up path?" — i.e. enough scope that it deserves its own task with its own design discussion. The round-3 entry sized the whole Shopping List as ~1.5 weeks; adding geofencing inflates the scope to ~3 weeks easily, doubling the time-to-market for the core value (the aisle-grouped list) without doubling the user impact. **Why "location-based reminders is Tier-2"** in the round-3 consultant-pass framing: the differentiator the round-3 entry called out is *combining* ingredients across recipes and grouping by aisle — the website cannot do this. Geofenced reminders are nice-to-have but not differentiating; every grocery / list / reminder app on the App Store has them. v1 ships the differentiator; v1.x adds the polish. **Activation trigger:** the deferral graduates to a real task when (i) the v1 list ships AND (ii) a user signal emerges from app reviews / dad's personal use — "I keep forgetting to look at the list when I'm at the store" — at which point the deferred design discussion above becomes the seed for the graduation. Until then, the user opens the app and taps the Saved tab → Shopping list when they want to see the list; the "Share to my spouse via iMessage" path per AC-39.7 covers the "hand the list to whoever's at the store" use case for v1. **What CL-68 does NOT defer:** the AC-39.7 share path (iMessage / AirDrop / Mail surface via `UIActivityViewController`) — that's the v1-shipped sharing surface and the round-3 capture's stated "primary export" affordance. The deferral is specifically the *push notification when geofence enters* affordance, not the share-the-list affordance. **What CL-68 does NOT add to the App Privacy questionnaire:** because the feature is deferred, the questionnaire stays as-is — no Location category is added in v1 of the Shopping List feature. (When the deferred task graduates, the questionnaire and constitution §9 both need a paired amendment.) **Considered:** ship a "remind me later" time-trigger notification instead of a location-trigger ("remind me at 5 PM today about my shopping list") as a lighter scope. Rejected for v1 — also requires `UNUserNotificationCenter` authorization (admittedly a path AC-36.1 already exposes a toggle for, but the toggle is currently UI-only per T-630 with the actual APNs wire-up deferred to T-631), and the v1 use case (dad puts the list together over the weekend, shares to mom via iMessage when she's heading to the store) doesn't need it. If T-631 lands and surfaces notification authorization, a future Tier-2 task can layer a time-trigger reminder on top without rebuilding the foundation. **Considered:** integrate with the iOS Reminders app via EventKit so the list shows up in the user's existing reminders surface. Rejected — adds an EventKit dependency, requires another permission prompt (Reminders), and forks the list's source of truth between SwiftData and EventKit (sync logic is hard). The `UIActivityViewController` share-as-text path can be routed to the Reminders app today via the user manually pasting it into a new reminder, which covers the same use case without the integration complexity. **Spec/trace:** none — this CL documents the deferral so a future contributor knows the design discussion happened. No AC reference because no v1 AC is created for the deferred surface. Implementing PR: T-680 (this spec PR; the deferral itself is the deliverable, not an implementation).

- **CL-69 (US-39 / T-681..T-689, 2026-05-27)** — **List lifecycle: single global list for v1, not multiple named lists. Extension path to named lists is documented for a future Tier-2 task.** Two candidate shapes: **(a) Single global list** — the user has one shopping list; "Add to shopping list" always appends to it; "Clear all" wipes it. The list is implicit and unnamed. **(b) Multiple named lists** — the user can have multiple lists ("Sunday dinner", "Camping trip", "Weekly groceries"); the user picks which list to add to at the per-recipe insertion step; each list has its own clear / share / aisle-grouping. **Decision: ship (a) for v1.** **Why (a):** the round-3 capture framed the use case as "add to shopping list" (singular) and the core differentiator is the aisle-grouping + cross-recipe combining, not the multi-list management. Multi-list management is a power-user feature that adds (i) a list-picker UI at every insertion site, (ii) a list-management screen with rename/delete affordances, (iii) a "which list am I looking at?" indicator on the rendering view, (iv) a SwiftData relationship (each `ShoppingListItem` references a `ShoppingList` parent), (v) an "is the empty state per-list or global?" decision (per-list means we need to render the list-picker even when no lists exist yet, which inverts the empty-state pattern), (vi) a "what happens to a list's items when the list is deleted?" decision (cascade vs. orphan). Each of those is a real design call that deserves a discussion, and none of them is the v1 differentiator. v1 ships single. **Why NOT (b):** the dominant use case the round-3 entry described — "send the list to a spouse on the way to the store" — is satisfied by single. The user adds the recipes they're planning for the week, the list rolls up, they share. If they decide mid-week to add a different recipe, the new ingredients append to the same list. If they want to start fresh, they tap Clear all. The "I have two parallel shopping trips planned" multi-list use case is a real but secondary scenario; if dad's actual use surfaces it, the deferred task graduates. **Activation trigger for (a) → (b):** the deferral graduates when dad's use surfaces a "I wish I could keep my camping list separate from my weekly groceries" pain point, OR the L5 / analytics show a usage pattern that benefits from segmentation (e.g. users clearing the list more than ~3 times a week, which would suggest they're using the single list to multiplex parallel trips and would benefit from being able to keep both lists open). The activation work is bounded: add a `ShoppingList` `@Model` with a name + createdAt; add a `parent: ShoppingList` relationship to `ShoppingListItem`; add a list-picker sheet at every "Add to shopping list" insertion point; add a list-management screen accessed from the existing Shopping List screen's nav bar; migrate the v1 single global list into a system-default `"My shopping list"` list (lightweight migration, additive). The migration shape is the reason this is a clean extension path rather than a v1 trap — the v1 schema's `ShoppingListItem` can gain a `parent` relationship without breaking the v1 data (every existing row gets attached to the default list during the upgrade migration). **Why the schema isn't pre-built for (b):** YAGNI — adding the `ShoppingList` parent now means designing the list-picker UX even though it ships hidden, which defeats the scope discipline. The CL-74 schema discussion documents the v1 shape as `ShoppingListItem` rows with no parent reference, and explicitly notes the extension path. **Considered:** ship (b) but auto-create a single default list so the v1 UX is identical to (a). Rejected — the schema cost is real (a new `@Model` class, a new relationship, a migration), and the "auto-create default" pattern is the same friction as "ship (a) and add the list-picker layer later." Either way the v1 user sees a single unnamed list; doing (b)'s scaffolding now without exposing it is dead code. **Considered:** ship (a) but call the list "Untitled" so the future (b) extension feels less abrupt. Rejected — naming an unnamed thing as "Untitled" is worse UX than leaving it nameless ("Untitled" implies the user forgot to name it; nameless implies it's just the list). The screen's nav title is `"Shopping list"` per AC-39.7's share header — that's the v1 identity, no per-list name. **Considered:** ship a "history" list that auto-archives the previous list when the user shares (so they can re-open a recent list). Rejected — feature creep; the v1 Share path is one-shot, and the user can re-add recipes if they want to re-build a list. **Spec/trace:** US-39 / AC-39.1 (empty state — global), AC-39.6 (clear all — global), AC-39.7 (share — global header). Implementing PR: T-682 (the persistence shape) + T-684 (the view + view-model). The deferral is implicit in the v1 schema's "no parent relationship" decision.

- **CL-70 (US-39 / T-681..T-689, 2026-05-27)** — **Quantity merging across recipes: per-recipe rows for v1, no quantity arithmetic. Documented future path for normalized-unit summation when (a) the static aisle classifier graduates to a tagged dictionary AND (b) a unit-normalization library has been audited.** The round-3 entry hinted at quantity handling without resolving it: if three recipes each call for "2 cups diced yellow onion" and a fourth calls for "1 cup chopped onion", should the list show "1 row of yellow onion (8 cups total)" or "4 separate rows"? **Candidate strategies:** **(a) Per-recipe rows, no merging** — every ingredient line becomes one `ShoppingListItem` row labeled with its source recipe. Three "2 cups diced yellow onion" entries produce three rows in the Produce aisle, each carrying its recipe-title sub-label per AC-39.2. **(b) Same-unit summation** — same-named ingredients with the same unit sum: "2 cups diced yellow onion" + "2 cups diced yellow onion" → "4 cups diced yellow onion". Different units stay split. **(c) Cross-unit normalization** — same-named ingredients in compatible units convert: "2 cups onion" + "8 oz onion" → "2.5 cups onion" (after a density-aware conversion). Different ingredients (yellow onion vs red onion) stay split. **(d) Quantity-string concatenation** — same-named ingredients keep their original quantity strings and just concat: "2 cups + 1 cup" rendered as a single row's quantity. **Decision: ship (a) — per-recipe rows, zero merging — for v1.** **Why (a):** parsing the quantity-and-unit prefix robustly is a hard problem the round-3 capture didn't size for. Real ingredient lines on dutchovendaddy.com include: `"2 cups diced yellow onion"`, `"1 medium yellow onion, diced"`, `"½ large red onion, thinly sliced"`, `"1 (15 oz) can crushed tomatoes"`, `"1 ½ pounds boneless skinless chicken thighs"`, `"1 sprig fresh rosemary"`. A robust parser handles fractions (½, ¼, 1½), descriptors-as-counts ("medium" / "large" — what's a "medium" onion in cups?), can-prefix ("1 (15 oz) can"), and inline-comma qualifiers (", diced"). The state of the art for unit-normalized recipe-ingredient parsing is a research-grade NLP problem; existing open-source libraries (e.g. `ingredient-phrase-tagger`, `recipe-scrapers`) are Python and aren't direct ports. Building a robust Swift parser is a multi-week task in itself, gates the v1 ship, and produces a result that's still wrong on enough edge cases that the user will see "6.25 cups onion" rendered as "6 ¼ cups" without understanding the conversion. **Why (a) and not (b):** even same-unit summation requires the quantity-prefix regex to handle fractions correctly (`½ cup + ½ cup = 1 cup` — easy; `½ cup + ¼ cup = ¾ cup` — needs fraction arithmetic; `1 ½ cups + ½ cup = 2 cups` — needs mixed-number normalization). Once you've written that, you've built half of (c)'s normalization anyway, but you're still wrong on the descriptor-as-count edge cases ("1 medium onion + 1 medium onion = 2 medium onions" is correct but the user probably wanted "2 medium onions" formatted as "2 onions"). The user's mental model is "I'm at the store, here are the things I need from this aisle"; per-recipe rows with the recipe-title sub-label match that mental model better than fragile arithmetic, because the user can see "I need onion for two recipes" and decide for themselves how much onion to buy. **Why (a) and not (c):** (c) is (b) plus a density-aware unit converter for volume-to-weight conversions ("1 cup flour" → 120g; "1 cup butter" → 227g). The conversion tables are ingredient-specific and the source-of-truth data isn't bundled with iOS; we'd need a dependency or our own table. Strike (c) hard. **Why (a) and not (d):** quantity-string concatenation produces visually ugly output ("2 cups + 1 cup + ½ cup diced yellow onion") that's worse than three clean rows; the user gets neither the convenience of a single computed number nor the clarity of separate rows. Strike (d). **What per-recipe rows look like in the rendered list:** each row shows the original ingredient text (verbatim from `RecipeIngredient.text`) followed by a small dimmer sub-label `(<recipe title>)` per AC-39.2. Three "diced yellow onion" entries from three recipes render as three Produce-aisle rows, each with a different recipe title in parens. This is a feature, not a bug — the user can mentally roll up the quantities at the store ("OK, three recipes call for onion — I'll grab two big yellow onions and call it good"). **Why the recipe-title sub-label is load-bearing:** without it, three identical "diced yellow onion" rows in Produce look like a bug ("why is onion showing three times?"). With the sub-label the user understands the multiplication source. **Activation trigger for (a) → (b)/(c):** the deferral graduates when (i) the v1 list ships AND (ii) the static aisle classifier graduates to (b) per CL-67 (because the tagged-ingredient dictionary surfaces a canonical-name pass that quantity merging needs to identify same-ingredient rows), AND (iii) the user signal emerges ("I wish my onion entries combined"). Without all three, the v1 per-recipe rendering is the right answer. The Swift-side libraries to audit when this graduates: `Apple Foundation Measurement` (handles unit conversions for distance / mass / volume but not ingredient-density conversions), `swift-fraction` or similar (no canonical choice; the constitution §3 "default answer is no new dependency" applies — the activation task likely re-implements fraction arithmetic in pure Swift). **Considered:** ship (a) but render the rows in an alphabetical-within-aisle order so duplicates visually cluster. Rejected — alphabetical-within-aisle is already what AC-39.4 says (the per-aisle order is sub-sorted by `addedAt` descending so the most recently added rows surface at the top; if dad adds three recipes in a row and they each call for onion, those three onion rows naturally cluster because they were added at adjacent timestamps). The user gets the visual cluster effect for free without a sort-key change. **Considered:** show a roll-up at the bottom of each aisle section ("3 onion entries"). Rejected — adds rendering complexity for marginal value; the visual cluster of three rows is the rollup. **Spec/trace:** US-39 / AC-39.2 (insert per ingredient, one row per source), AC-39.4 (aisle rendering with the per-recipe rows), AC-39.5 (per-row toggle). Implementing PR: T-682 (persistence) + T-684 (rendering).

- **CL-71 (US-39 / T-684 / T-685, 2026-05-27) — DEFERRED PANTRY INVENTORY** — **Per-row "I already have this" toggle is session-scoped (resets only on a deliberate user action — toggle again or Clear all — never automatically); no persistent pantry inventory in v1. Documented future path to a real pantry-inventory feature gated on a separate task.** The round-3 entry called out "I already have this" toggle per ingredient as a v1 feature. Two interpretations: **(a) Per-row session toggle** — the toggle's check state lives on the `ShoppingListItem` row (`isChecked: Bool`). Checking a row strikes it through visually and the row stays in the list (the user might want to un-check if they got home and realized they were wrong); the row clears only when the user taps the row's toggle again or hits Clear all. There is no app-wide memory of "user already has olive oil." **(b) Persistent pantry inventory** — a separate `PantryItem` `@Model` records "user always has olive oil, garlic, salt, pepper, butter, etc."; when the user adds a recipe to the shopping list, any ingredient that matches a pantry entry is auto-checked (pre-marked as "I have this"). The user can edit the pantry independently of the shopping list (add / remove items, set always-have vs. usually-have). **Decision: ship (a) for v1. Pantry inventory is deferred.** **Why (a):** matches the round-3 entry's literal framing ("an 'I already have this' toggle per ingredient" — singular, per-ingredient, in-context) without the heavyweight scaffolding (b) requires. The check state persists across app launches (because the row is in SwiftData) but doesn't carry across lists or recipe-add events — every new "Add to shopping list" tap inserts fresh `isChecked: false` rows even if the user had already checked a different row for the same ingredient earlier. The user's mental model is "I'm working a single list, I check things off as I confirm I have them, I clear the list when I'm done"; that model maps directly to (a)'s shape. **Why NOT (b) in v1:** pantry inventory is its own feature with its own design discussion: how is the pantry populated initially? (Empty, walking through the user's pantry one item at a time, importing from common-staples preset?) How does pantry matching work? (Exact string match on the ingredient text, fuzzy match, longest-prefix on the aisle-tagged dictionary?) What happens when the user runs out of olive oil — is there an "I'm out of this" affordance that adds the pantry item back to the shopping list automatically? Does the pantry have categories (always-have vs. usually-have vs. sometimes-have)? Does the pantry have quantity / expiration tracking? Each of those is a real design call, and none is the v1 differentiator. The round-3 size estimate of "~1.5 weeks" for the whole Shopping List feature does not accommodate a pantry feature. (b) is its own ~2-week task. **Activation trigger for (a) → (b):** the deferral graduates when (i) the v1 list ships AND (ii) dad's use surfaces the "I'm tired of un-checking olive oil every time I build a list" pain point, OR analytics surface a usage pattern showing a small set of ingredients are consistently checked on every list (a strong signal those ingredients belong in a pantry inventory). The activation task is bounded but real: a new `PantryItem` `@Model`, a new Settings screen ("Manage pantry"), a matcher in the "Add to shopping list" flow, an "I'm out of this — add to list" reverse-affordance, and the L1 tests around matching. The v1 schema is compatible (the new `PantryItem` is additive to a future SchemaV5; the v1 `ShoppingListItem` rows are unaffected). **Why "session-scoped only in semantic" but "persisted across launches":** the AC-39.5 wording is subtle. The check state is persisted on the row (the row itself is in SwiftData; checked rows stay checked after a relaunch), but the check semantic doesn't carry across recipe-add events or across list-clear events. "Session" here means "the current shopping trip's worth of list activity, however long that takes" — which might be hours, days, or weeks; the row's `isChecked` state is a property of *that row*, not of *that ingredient as an abstract concept the user owns*. (b) is what'd give you "that ingredient as an abstract concept the user owns." (a) gives you "this row, in this list, in this trip." **Visual strikethrough treatment:** checked rows render with `.strikethrough(true)` on the ingredient text + the sub-label, and the foreground color shifts from `DODColor.label` to `DODColor.labelSecondary` so the row visually recedes. The toggle's icon flips from `circle` to `checkmark.circle.fill`. The row remains in the list (no auto-hide) so the user can un-check if they realized they were wrong. **Considered:** auto-remove checked rows after a delay (5 seconds of remaining checked → row deletes from the list). Rejected — the user might tap a row by accident and then re-check the list and find the row gone with no recovery path. Strikethrough + retain is the safer pattern. **Considered:** add a "Hide checked" toggle at the top of the list so the user can collapse the strikethrough rows. Rejected for v1 — adds a UI control for a use case (visual clutter from many checked rows) that may not emerge in real use; if the v1 list never grows past ~30 rows in practice (a reasonable upper bound for one cooking week), the strikethrough rows aren't visually overwhelming. Future Tier-2 if user signal emerges. **Considered:** make the check action also delete the row from the list (since the user said they have it, why keep the row?). Rejected — same recovery-path argument; strikethrough + retain preserves the user's ability to undo. **Spec/trace:** US-39 / AC-39.5 (toggle semantics + persistence shape), AC-39.10 (no telemetry on toggle — the check action is private, no event fires). Implementing PR: T-684 (view + view-model).

- **CL-72 (US-39 / T-686, 2026-05-27)** — **Share format: plain text via `UIActivityViewController`; no `MessageUI` framework dependency, no URL-encoded structured payload.** Three candidate share surfaces: **(a) `UIActivityViewController` with a plain-text item** — the system share sheet wraps a single `String` and surfaces iMessage, AirDrop, Mail, Notes, Copy, third-party apps. Zero new framework dependency (`UIActivityViewController` is part of UIKit, already linked). **(b) `MessageUI.MFMessageComposeViewController`** — the dedicated iMessage compose surface; opens directly in the Messages app's compose view; no other share targets visible from inside the app. Adds a `MessageUI` framework dependency. **(c) URL-encoded structured payload** — a custom URL scheme (`dod-shopping://share?aisles=produce:onion,garlic|dairy:milk&recipes=123,456`) that other apps could in theory decode if they registered the scheme; the user shares the URL via `UIActivityViewController` and the receiver pastes it back into the app. **Decision: ship (a) — `UIActivityViewController` with plain-text — for v1.** **Why (a):** the round-3 capture said "Share-via-iMessage as the primary export (perfect for sending the list to a spouse on the way to the store)." iMessage is one target among many that `UIActivityViewController` surfaces; (a) gives the user iMessage AND every other share path (AirDrop to a partner's iPad, Mail to themselves for archival, Notes to keep a record, Copy to paste into any other app). (a) costs us zero new framework dependency (UIKit's `UIActivityViewController` is already linked transitively from SwiftUI), zero new permissions, zero new App Privacy questionnaire entries. **Why NOT (b):** `MessageUI` is a framework cost we don't need — the share sheet already surfaces iMessage. And `MFMessageComposeViewController` is *more* restrictive than (a) on the user's choice (no AirDrop, no Mail, no Notes, no Copy from the same affordance — the user would have to back out and choose a different button for a different surface). (b) inverts the user's affordance: instead of one "Share" button that opens to a menu of targets, you'd need either "Share to iMessage" (one button) plus a separate "Share elsewhere" button (defeats the simplification (b) is trying to provide), or just "Share to iMessage" with everything else removed (a regression for the user who wants AirDrop). **Why NOT (c):** URL-encoded payloads are ugly to look at, the receiver almost certainly doesn't have the app installed (the v1 user base is a single shared-household installation: dad's phone, the user is sharing to mom's phone, mom doesn't have the app), and the structured payload doesn't survive copy-paste from iMessage to Notes to Mail without rendering as an inscrutable string. (c) is the wrong shape for the v1 use case where the receiver is not running the app. **Plain-text format per AC-39.7:** the share payload is deterministic and human-readable: a one-line header `"Shopping list — May 27, 2026"`, one blank line, then per-aisle blocks. Each aisle block starts with the aisle name (e.g. `"Produce"`) on its own line, then one `- <ingredient text> (<recipe title>)` line per row. Aisles render in the AC-39.4 store-walk order. Example: ```\nShopping list — May 27, 2026\n\nProduce\n- 2 cups diced yellow onion (Sunday Pot Roast)\n- 1 sprig fresh rosemary (Sunday Pot Roast)\n\nDairy\n- 1 cup heavy cream (Chocolate Pots de Crème)\n\nPantry\n- 1 cup all-purpose flour (Buttermilk Biscuits)\n``` **Why include the recipe title on every row in the share text:** the receiver (mom at the store) gets context that helps her think about substitutions ("I can't find fresh rosemary, is this for Sunday's pot roast? I'll grab dried rosemary instead"). Without the recipe title, every row is decontextualized — the receiver has to guess which recipe needs which ingredient. **Why the share text includes checked rows (not just unchecked):** the share-time snapshot is "this is the full shopping list as it stands"; the receiver might have a different mental model of which items are "already had" vs "need to buy" (the receiver doesn't have the same pantry as the sender), and stripping checked rows pre-emptively biases the shared view in a way the sender may not have intended. The sender can Clear-then-share or strikethrough-then-share if they want to send only the unchecked subset; for v1 the simplest contract is "share what you see." **Why a one-line header with a date:** the receiver might receive the list days after the sender sent it; the date helps disambiguate ("oh this was last weekend's list, today's is different"). The header copy `"Shopping list — <date>"` is short enough to read at a glance and signals the message's intent immediately. **Considered:** share-as-image (render the list to a `UIImage` via `ImageRenderer` and share that). Rejected — images are bigger payloads, can't be edited by the receiver if they want to add their own items, can't be searched / pasted / copy-modified. Plain text is the right shape for a "list" content type. **Considered:** share as a `.txt` file attachment instead of inline text. Rejected — attachments add friction (the receiver has to tap to open) and inline-text + Notes / Copy paths cover the "archive a copy" use case without the attachment overhead. **Considered:** offer the user a choice of formats at share time (plain text vs. markdown vs. CSV). Rejected — over-engineering for v1; plain text is the lowest-common-denominator format that every receiver app handles. **Considered:** include a footer with a link to dutchovendaddy.com or a per-recipe link for each block. Rejected — the link would route the receiver to the website (not the app), adds noise to the share payload, and could be perceived as promotional. The recipe-title parenthetical is enough attribution. **Spec/trace:** US-39 / AC-39.7 (the share path), AC-39.10 (the `shoppingListShared(itemCount:)` event that fires when the share sheet presents — counted at the moment of share, not at the moment of any specific target tap). Implementing PR: T-686 (`feat/T-686-shopping-list-share`).

- **CL-73 (US-39 / T-684 / T-685, 2026-05-27)** — **Entry surfaces for the Shopping List feature: Saved tab toolbar + per-row long-press menu + RecipeDetail nav-bar action. Three entry points cover the v1 use cases.** The user reaches the Shopping List feature in three places: **(1) Saved tab toolbar** — a new `cart` toolbar icon on the leading edge of `SavedView`'s nav bar opens `ShoppingListView`. This is the canonical "I want to see / edit my list" entry point. The Saved tab is the home for "my saved stuff," and the shopping list is "my saved stuff in service of a shopping trip" — same mental neighborhood. **(2) Per-recipe long-press menu (Saved tab context)** — per AC-39.3, the existing per-card long-press menu established by US-34 / AC-34.1 gains a new "Add to shopping list" item (with the `cart.badge.plus` glyph) **on Saved-tab cards only**. The Feed and Search tabs' cards do NOT get this menu item in v1 — the entry surface is bounded to Saved because the user's mental model is "I'm adding from my saved recipes, not from random Feed posts." (A user CAN add a Feed-tab post by tapping in and using the recipe-detail nav-bar action per (3) below; the long-press menu is a power-user convenience for already-saved recipes.) **(3) RecipeDetail nav-bar action** — a new `cart.badge.plus` button on the trailing edge of `RecipeDetailView`'s toolbar group, between Save (AC-4.7), Download (AC-35.1), and Share (AC-4.8). This is the primary discovery surface — a user reading a recipe taps the cart button and the ingredients are appended to their list. Unlike (1)+(2), this entry surface is available on ANY recipe-kind post (US-37 / AC-37.2 `.recipe`), not just saved ones; the user can add a Feed-tab post's ingredients to their shopping list without saving the post first. (Articles per US-37 / AC-37.3 don't have ingredients, so the button is hidden on `ArticleDetailView`.) **Why three entry points and not one:** the round-3 entry didn't enumerate surfaces — the deliverable is "the user can build a shopping list." Three different access patterns emerge from real use: (a) the recipe-rending user who wants to add a recipe they're currently reading (RecipeDetail nav-bar), (b) the planning user who's browsing their saved tab and wants to multi-select recipes for the week (Saved-tab toolbar + bulk path per AC-39.3, or per-card long-press), (c) the in-progress user who wants to check what's already on the list (Saved-tab toolbar → ShoppingListView). Three surfaces cover three workflows without overloading any single one. **Why NOT a tab of its own:** Shopping List isn't differentiated enough from "saved stuff" to warrant a top-level tab slot — tab-bar real estate is constitutionally bounded (the existing four tabs per AC-16.1 are Recipes & Articles / Categories / Saved / Search, and adding a fifth would force the tab-bar truncation problem T-660 just fixed back into the conversation). The Saved-tab toolbar entry handles the "main entry point" use case without the tab-bar inflation. **Why NOT a Settings-page row:** Settings is for app-level preferences (notifications toggle, appearance picker, share-format default) per US-36; the shopping list is data, not a preference. Routing through Settings would be a category mismatch. **Why NOT a Feed-tab long-press menu item:** the Feed tab's cards represent "discoverable content from dutchovendaddy.com" — adding a Feed post's ingredients to a shopping list implies the user is committing to cook that post, which is a stronger signal than "I'm browsing." If they're committing, they probably want to save the recipe too (so they can find it again before the trip); the natural flow is "tap into the recipe, save AND add to shopping list" — both nav-bar actions are right there. If user feedback surfaces a "I want to add to list without opening" pattern, a Feed-tab long-press menu can graduate later. **What the Saved-tab toolbar button looks like:** SF Symbol `cart`, accessibility label "Shopping list", leading edge of the trailing-edge toolbar group (so a future "Build shopping list" multi-select button per AC-39.3 can sit adjacent without crowding the existing toolbar items). When the list is non-empty, a small `Text(\"\(count)\")` badge can decorate the icon for at-a-glance count — this is a Tier-2 follow-up if user signal surfaces; v1 ships without the badge to keep the toolbar visual identity stable. **Considered:** route everything through a single share-sheet-style picker that pops up from a single new tab. Rejected — the round-3 capture's spirit is "the recipe app you cook from becomes the grocery list you shop from"; that implies the shopping list is woven into the recipe surfaces, not a standalone tool. Three entry points reflects the integration. **Considered:** ship just the RecipeDetail nav-bar action for v1; defer the Saved-tab entry surfaces. Rejected — the user needs a way to *view* the list once it's been built; without the Saved-tab toolbar entry, the only path to the list is "tap into a recipe and the cart action shows the existing list" which is indirect. The Saved-tab toolbar is the minimum-viable view affordance. **Considered:** include the per-card long-press menu addition on Feed and Search tabs too (so any list-render surface can add a card to the shopping list). Rejected for v1 — bounded scope; the dominant use case is "I'm planning from my saved recipes." If user signal surfaces the Feed/Search pattern, it's a small follow-up. **Spec/trace:** US-39 / AC-39.2 (RecipeDetail entry), AC-39.3 (Saved-tab bulk + long-press), AC-39.9 (iPad sidebar entry — same Saved-tab origin). Implementing PR: T-685 (`feat/T-685-shopping-list-entry-surfaces`).

- **CL-74 (US-39 / T-682, 2026-05-27)** — **Persistence shape: a new `ShoppingListItem` `@Model` class in `DODPersistence`, added to a new `SchemaV4` stage. This is the canonical "add a new model" SwiftData migration case — distinct from the "add an optional column to an existing model" trap T-640 hit.** The Shopping List feature needs SwiftData persistence (AC-39.8). Three candidate shapes: **(a) New `ShoppingListItem` `@Model` class, V3 → V4 stage** — `ShoppingListItem` joins the schema models array as a brand-new entity. Stage is `.lightweight(fromVersion: SchemaV3.self, toVersion: SchemaV4.self)` because no existing entity is touched. **(b) Extend an existing entity** — add a `shoppingListAddedAt: Date?` column to `CachedRecipe` and a separate column for the ingredient-line shopping state. Reuses existing entity shape; piggybacks on the cached-recipe row's lifecycle. **(c) Plain UserDefaults / JSON file** — bypass SwiftData entirely; persist the shopping list as a JSON-encoded array in a file under the app's documents directory. **Decision: ship (a) — new `@Model` class + V4 schema stage — for v1.** **Why (a) and not the T-640 trap:** T-640 (US-37 article-body column) tried to add an optional column (`articleBodyHTML: String?`) to an EXISTING `@Model` (`CachedRecipe`) by introducing a SchemaV4 that referenced the same `CachedRecipe.self` with the new column. That collided because SwiftData computes the schema checksum from the `@Model` class shape — and SchemaV3's `CachedRecipe.self` (without the column) and SchemaV4's `CachedRecipe.self` (with the column) produce the SAME checksum (because the @Model class shape that the checksum hashes is the runtime class, not the schema entry's snapshot of it). The fix was to bump the model's schema identity in place (Apple's recommended pattern uses an alias type), but T-640's pragmatic fall-back was to add the column inline on the existing class and rely on SwiftData's default schema-inference migration to accept the new optional. **The Shopping List case is fundamentally different**: we are adding a BRAND-NEW `@Model` class (`ShoppingListItem`) that did not exist in any prior schema. The new class introduces a NEW schema identity (a new entity name, a new checksum derived from its new class shape) and SchemaV4 declares `models: [...CachedRecipe.self, CachedListPage.self, ..., ShoppingListItem.self]` — the new entry doesn't clash with itself because it doesn't exist in V3. The V3 → V4 lightweight migration adds the entity, leaves every existing entity untouched, and produces zero rows in the new table at upgrade time (the user populates it organically by adding to their list). **Why (a) and not (b):** extending `CachedRecipe` couples the shopping-list state to the cached-recipe row's lifecycle, which is wrong — `CachedRecipe` is subject to LRU eviction (NFR-1, NFR-2), so a recipe the user added to their shopping list could get evicted out from under their list. (b) forces a "pin the recipe row whenever it has shopping-list state" rule that's the same shape as the existing AC-5.2 / AC-35.5 pinning rule for saves / downloads — three pin reasons on one row instead of two. The proliferation of pin-reasons is its own complexity. Strike (b). **Why (a) and not (c):** SwiftData is the project's canonical persistence layer per constitution §3 and §5 — "SwiftData for local cache and saved recipes. Plain `Codable` + file storage acceptable for simple key-value needs." A list of shopping-list rows is structured, queried, sortable, and growable; that's not a "simple key-value need." SwiftData is the right tool. (c) would force the view-model to re-decode the file on every render, would defeat SwiftData's `@Query` reactive binding (the view-model would need its own observation infrastructure), and would orphan the shopping list from the rest of the persistence story. Strike (c). **`ShoppingListItem` schema shape:** ```swift\n@Model\npublic final class ShoppingListItem {\n  public var recipeID: Int            // links to CachedRecipe.id (no relationship — see below)\n  public var recipeTitle: String      // captured at insertion time (the sub-label per AC-39.2)\n  public var ingredientText: String   // verbatim from RecipeIngredient.text\n  public var aisleRaw: String         // Aisle enum raw value (\"produce\" / \"dairy\" / etc.)\n  public var isChecked: Bool          // AC-39.5 toggle state\n  public var addedAt: Date            // for AC-39.4 sort order\n  public init(recipeID: Int, recipeTitle: String, ingredientText: String, aisle: Aisle, isChecked: Bool = false, addedAt: Date = .now) {\n    self.recipeID = recipeID\n    self.recipeTitle = recipeTitle\n    self.ingredientText = ingredientText\n    self.aisleRaw = aisle.rawValue\n    self.isChecked = isChecked\n    self.addedAt = addedAt\n  }\n  public var aisle: Aisle { Aisle(rawValue: aisleRaw) ?? .other }\n}\n``` **Why no relationship to `CachedRecipe`:** the simpler `recipeID: Int` foreign-key-style reference is the right shape. A SwiftData `@Relationship` to `CachedRecipe` would (a) couple the shopping-list row's lifecycle to the cached-recipe row (deletion cascade rules become a real design call), (b) require a fetch-with-relationship path in the view model, (c) re-introduce the eviction trap from (b) above — if `CachedRecipe` is LRU-evicted, the relationship snaps. Storing `recipeID: Int` keeps the shopping-list row self-contained; the only thing it needs from `CachedRecipe` is the title (captured at insertion time into `recipeTitle: String`) and the eventual analytics payload (the recipe ID itself). If the cached recipe is evicted later, the shopping-list row continues to render correctly (the title is on the row, not behind a relationship). **Why the `aisleRaw: String` storage instead of a SwiftData-native enum:** SwiftData's enum support via `@Attribute` is acceptable but introduces a schema-identity concern when the enum gains new cases (every new case is a schema bump). Storing the raw string and providing a computed `aisle: Aisle { Aisle(rawValue: aisleRaw) ?? .other }` accessor is the lower-coupling shape: adding a new `Aisle` case is a `DODDomain` change with zero persistence-side migration cost. The `.other` fallback in the computed accessor is defensive — a future schema rollback could leave a row with an `aisleRaw` string the current `Aisle` enum doesn't recognize, and routing it to `.other` is safer than crashing. **Migration test:** T-682 ships an L1 test `MigrationV4Tests.V3_to_V4_lightweightMigration` that opens a SchemaV3 container, inserts a `CachedRecipe` + a `CachedListPage` + a `CachedImage`, closes it, re-opens at SchemaV4, asserts (i) every V3 row is intact and queryable, (ii) the new `ShoppingListItem` entity exists and is empty, and (iii) an insertion of a `ShoppingListItem` round-trips through `try modelContext.save()`. Mirrors the existing `MigrationV3Tests.V2_to_V3_lightweightMigration` shape per MIGRATION.md rule 3. **What CL-74 explicitly says is NOT acceptable:** reusing an existing column on `CachedRecipe` for shopping-list state. Reusing `ingredientsJSON` (the cached JSON-LD ingredient blob) as a generic text-body slot was rejected by CL-63 for article bodies; the same anti-pattern applies here. Each domain concept gets its own column or its own entity. **Considered:** introduce a parent `ShoppingList` entity now (as a forward-compat lever for CL-69's deferred multi-list path) with a default singleton list. Rejected — YAGNI; the v1 schema is single-list-implicit, the future migration (adding the parent and back-filling every existing row into a system-default list) is bounded and additive. **Considered:** store the `Aisle` enum as a SwiftData `@Attribute(.transformable)` value. Rejected — adds an `NSSecureCoding` requirement on `Aisle` and serializes to a `Data` blob, which is opaque vs. the string raw-value (you can inspect a SwiftData store with a debugger and see "produce" / "dairy"; you can't with a transformable blob). String raw-value is the readable shape. **Considered:** add a `sourceRecipeURL: URL` column for future "tap an item to go to the recipe" affordance. Rejected for v1 — the `recipeID` already enables that lookup against `CachedRecipe.canonicalURL`; storing the URL redundantly invites drift. Future Tier-2 if the affordance graduates. **Spec/trace:** US-39 / AC-39.8 (persistence contract). Implementing PR: T-682 (`feat/T-682-shopping-list-persistence`).

- **CL-75 (US-39 / T-687, 2026-05-27)** — **Analytics: two new events (`shoppingListItemAdded(recipeID:aisle:)` and `shoppingListShared(itemCount:)`) join the constitution §9 allowlist. Aisle is a coarse-grained ~9-value enum string; no raw ingredient text.
